we can simplify this equation to

\displaystyle 0=\cos(\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon})+\Big(2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}-2\frac{(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})\tau m}{m^{2}+(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})^{2}}\Big)\textup{sinc}(\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}).

(3.7)

Next, we use
m^{2}=\upsilon_{\xi}^{2}-\xi^{2}=-(\xi+\upsilon_{\xi})(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})
and
2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}=\sqrt{d-\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m}
to rewrite

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}-\frac{2(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})\tau m}{m^{2}+(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}-2\frac{(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})\tau m}{(-(\xi+\upsilon_{\xi})+(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi}))(\xi-\upsilon_{\xi})}

(3.8)

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}+\frac{\tau m}{\upsilon_{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}}\Big((2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi})^{2}+2\varepsilon\tau m\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{d-\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{2}-2\varepsilon\tau m}{2\varepsilon\upsilon_{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d-\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{2}-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m}}.

Plugging (
3.8
) into (
3.7
) gives us (
3.2
).

Now, we argue that the reverse direction is also true. If (
3.2
) is fulfilled, then using (
3.8
) implies that (
3.7
) is valid. Then, we fix a arbitrary
c_{3}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
. For this
c_{3}
there exists exactly one
c_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
such that (
3.6
) is satisfied. Furthermore, (
3.5
) is also true. The choices
w_{1}=c_{1}a_{+},w_{2}=\exp(B\varepsilon)\exp(-A\varepsilon)w_{1}
and
w_{3}=c_{3}a_{-}
yield with (
3.4
) that
u
defined by (
3.3
) is in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{N})=\textup{dom }H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi]
. Moreover, by construction
u\in(\ker H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi])\setminus\{0\}
which implies
0\in\sigma_{\textup{p}}(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi])
.

Step 2.

Consider the function
a_{0}:[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi)\mapsto[0,\infty)
,
a_{0}(u)=-u\cot(u)
.
It easy to see that
a_{0}
is continuous and
a_{0}([\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi))=[0,\infty)
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle a_{0}^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=-\cot(u)+\frac{u}{\sin^{2}(u)}=\frac{-\cos(u)\sin(u)+u}{\sin^{2}(u)}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\frac{1}{2}\sin(2u)+u}{\sin^{2}(u)}\geq\frac{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}}{\sin^{2}(u)}>0,\qquad u\in[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi),

and hence
a_{0}
is monotonically increasing and bijective. Thus, the same holds for its inverse function

u_{0}:=a_{0}^{-1}:[0,\infty)\mapsto[\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi).

For
a\in[0,\infty)
we have
a=-u_{0}(a)\cot(u_{0}(a))
and therefore multiplying with
\textup{sinc}(u_{0}(a))
shows that
u_{0}
fulfils the relation

\cos(u_{0}(a))+a\textup{sinc}(u_{0}(a))=0,\qquad a\in[0,\infty).

(3.9)

Step 3.
Now we use the function
u_{0}
from the previous step to find for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
a
\xi_{\varepsilon}
such that
0\in\sigma_{\textup{p}}(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}])
. We start by claiming that there exists an
a_{\varepsilon}>0
which fulfils

a_{\varepsilon}=\frac{d-u_{0}^{2}(a_{\varepsilon})-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-u_{0}^{2}(a_{\varepsilon})-4\varepsilon\tau m}}.

(3.10)

In fact, if we set

b_{\varepsilon}:=\sqrt{\min\{d,\pi^{2}\}-4\varepsilon(|\tau m|+1)}

and choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small we can guarantee
b_{\varepsilon}\in(\tfrac{\pi}{2},\pi)
, and hence
u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon})\in(0,\infty)
. Since
u_{0}
is monotonically increasing and continuous, the function

F:[0,u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon})]\to\mathbb{R},\quad F(a)=a-\frac{d-u_{0}^{2}(a)-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-u_{0}^{2}(a)-4\varepsilon\tau m}}

is continuous and well-defined. The properties of the function
u_{0}
and the assumption
d>\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}
show that

F(0)=-\frac{d-\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}-4\varepsilon\tau m}}

is smaller than zero for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Moreover, since
u_{0}^{-1}
is continuous and
\lim_{b\to\pi}u_{0}^{-1}(b)=\infty
, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}F(u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Bigl(u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon})-\frac{d-\min\{d,\pi^{2}\}+4\varepsilon(|\tau m|+1)-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-\min\{d,\pi^{2}\}+4\varepsilon(|\tau m|+1)-4\varepsilon\tau m}}\Bigr).

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}u_{0}^{-1}(\sqrt{d}),&d<\pi^{2},\\
\infty,&d\geq\pi^{2},\end{cases}

\displaystyle>0.

Thus,
F(u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon}))>0
if
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small.
Hence, there exists an
a_{\varepsilon}\in(0,u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon}))
such that (
3.10
) is fulfilled.

For this
a_{\varepsilon}
we also have

\displaystyle d-u_{0}^{2}(a_{\varepsilon})-4\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m

\displaystyle>d-u_{0}^{2}(u_{0}^{-1}(b_{\varepsilon}))-4\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m

\displaystyle=d-\min\{d,\pi^{2}\}+4\varepsilon(|\tau m|+1)-4\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m

\displaystyle>0

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Thus,

\xi_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\sqrt{d-u_{0}^{2}(a_{\varepsilon})-4\varepsilon^{2}m^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m}>0

(3.11)

is well-defined. From (
3.11
) and the definition of
\mu_{\xi,\varepsilon}
below (
3.2
) it follows that

u_{0}(a_{\varepsilon})=\sqrt{d-4\varepsilon^{2}\upsilon_{\xi_{\varepsilon}}^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m}=\mu_{\xi_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon},

and plugging this expression into (
3.10
) yields

a_{\varepsilon}=\frac{d-\mu_{\xi_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon}^{2}-2\varepsilon\tau m}{\sqrt{d-\mu_{\xi_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon}^{2}-4\varepsilon\tau m}}.

Combining these relations with (
3.9
) shows that
(
3.2
) is fulfilled for
\xi=\xi_{\varepsilon}
. Thus,
0\in\sigma_{\textup{p}}(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}])
by
Step 1
.

Step 4.

Finally, we show in this step
0\in\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}})
for
\varepsilon>0
chosen sufficiently small. This follows from
[
34
, Theorem XIII.85 (d)]
if we can show that for all
\delta>0
there is a
\gamma_{\delta}>0
such that
(-\delta,\delta)\cap\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi])\neq\emptyset
for all
\xi\in(\xi_{\varepsilon}-\gamma_{\delta},\xi_{\varepsilon}+\gamma_{\delta})
. We assume that our claim is not true. In this case there exists a
\delta^{\prime}>0
and a sequence
(\xi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\xi_{n}\rightarrow\xi_{\varepsilon}
for
n\to\infty
and
(-\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\cap\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{n}])=\emptyset
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\lVert{(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}]-z)^{-1}-(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{n}]-z)^{-1}}\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})}

\displaystyle={\lVert{(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}]-z)^{-1}\sigma_{2}(\xi_{n}-\xi_{\varepsilon})(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{n}]-z)^{-1}}\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(\textup{Im }z)^{2}}\lvert{\xi_{n}-\xi_{\varepsilon}}\rvert\rightarrow 0,\quad n\to\infty,

holds for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{n}]
converges in norm resolvent sense to
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}]
. Moreover,
(-\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\cap\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{n}])=\emptyset
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
[
32
, Theorem VIII.24 (a)]
imply the contradiction
(-\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\cap\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}[\xi_{\varepsilon}])=\emptyset
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

We note that in the situation of Theorem
3.2
one can even show
\sigma(H_{V_{\varepsilon}})=\mathbb{R}
for all
\varepsilon>0
which are sufficiently small; cf.
[
38
, Theorem 6.5]
. Furthermore, the statement of Theorem
3.2
, that
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
does not converge in the norm resolvent sense to
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
, remains true in more general situations, e.g., if
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, is as in Section
1.1
(
vii
) and contains a flat part, see

[
38
, Theorem 6.7]
for more details.

4.
Approximation of Dirac operators with
\delta
-shell potentials supported on rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graphs

The main aim of this section is to prove Theorem
2.1
for the case that
\Sigma
is a rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graph. In this case, there exist
\zeta\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
and
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
such that

\displaystyle\Omega_{+}

\displaystyle=\{\kappa(x^{\prime},x_{\theta}):(x^{\prime},x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\textup{ and }x_{\theta}<\zeta(x^{\prime})\},

(4.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{-}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\overline{\Omega_{+}}=\{\kappa(x^{\prime},x_{\theta}):(x^{\prime},x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\textup{ and }x_{\theta}>\zeta(x^{\prime})\},

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=\partial\Omega_{+}=\Sigma_{\zeta,\kappa}:=\{\kappa(x^{\prime},\zeta(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\}.

To emphasize the importance of this result, let us rephrase Theorem
2.1
for the special case of a rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graph.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graph, let
q\in L^{\infty}((-1,1);[0,\infty))
with
\int_{-1}^{1}q(s)\,ds=1
and assume that

\eta,\tau\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
satisfy the condition

\sup_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}d(x_{\Sigma})<\frac{\pi^{2}}{4},\qquad d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}.

(4.2)

Let
V
and
V_{\varepsilon}
be as in (
1.9
) and (
1.5
), and define
\widetilde{V}
by (
1.11
).
Then, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
there exist
C>0
and
\varepsilon_{3}\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
such that

{\lVert{(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)^{-1}}\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\quad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{3}).

In particular,
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
converges to
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
in the norm resolvent sense as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

This theorem is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.7
, Proposition
4.9
and Proposition
4.28
.

Section
4
is devoted to proving these propositions. We proceed as follows: In Section
4.1
we introduce various integral operators associated to the free Dirac operator. Then, in Section
4.2
we recall comparable resolvent formulas for
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
and
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
from
[
8
]
. Moreover, relying again on results from
[
8
]
we provide convergence results for the operators involved in these resolvent formulas and end the section by stating Proposition
4.7
which gives abstract conditions for the norm resolvent convergence of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
. Afterwards, we show in Section
4.3
and Section
4.4
(in Proposition
4.9
and Proposition
4.28
, respectively) that these conditions are met if (
4.2
) is fulfilled.

Finally, let us mention that the restriction to rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graphs is only necessary in Section
4.4
and all results from the Sections
4.1
–
4.3
remain valid for the general class of hypersurfaces described in Section
1.1
(
vii
).

4.1.
The free Dirac operator and associated integral operators

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and recall that the Dirac matrices
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta},\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are given by (
1.16
)–(
1.17
). The free Dirac operator
H
is the differential operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
given by

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:=-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta,\qquad\textup{dom }H:=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(4.3)

With the help of the Fourier transform one gets that
H
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\sigma(H)=\left(-\infty,-\lvert{m}\rvert\right]\cup\left[\lvert{m}\rvert,\infty\right)
, see for instance
[
10
, Section 2]
for
\theta=2
and
[
39
, Theorem 1.1]
for
\theta=3
. For
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(H)
we have

R_{z}u(x):=(H-z)^{-1}u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x-y)u(y)\,dy,\qquad u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),~x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta},

where
G_{z}
is given for
\theta=2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
by

\displaystyle G_{z}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}}{2\pi}K_{1}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\lvert{x}\rvert\big)\frac{\alpha\cdot x}{\lvert{x}\rvert}

(4.4)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\lvert{x}\rvert\big)\left(m\beta+zI_{2}\right)

and for
\theta=3
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
by

G_{z}(x)=\left(zI_{4}+m\beta+i\left(1-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\lvert{x}\rvert\right)\frac{\alpha\cdot x}{\lvert{x}\rvert^{2}}\right)\frac{e^{i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\lvert{x}\rvert}}{4\pi\lvert{x}\rvert};

(4.5)

cf., e.g.,
[
7
,
9
,
39
]
.
Here,
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
denote the modified Bessel functions of the second kind of order zero and one, respectively.
Note that
R_{z}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and it can also be viewed as a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

We move on to the discussion of potential and boundary integral operators associated with the free Dirac operator. In the following, let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
be fixed and let
\Omega_{\pm}
and
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
be as in (
4.1
). First, we introduce the potential operator
\Phi_{z}:L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{z}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma})\varphi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}),\qquad\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),~x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

(4.6)

We note that
\Phi_{z}
is indeed well-defined and bounded, see
[
2
, Lemma 2.1]
.
Further properties of
\Phi_{z}
are summarized in the following proposition. These results are well-known and a proof can be found in
[
8
, Appendix C]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
and let
\Phi_{z}
be given by (
4.6
). Then, the following is true:

(i)

For any
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
the operator
\Phi_{z}
gives rise to a bounded operator

\Phi_{z}:H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r+1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).\\

(ii)

For
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
one has
(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})(\Phi_{z}\varphi)_{\pm}=0
.

(iii)

The adjoint
\Phi_{z}^{*}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
of
\Phi_{z}
acts on
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as

\Phi_{z}^{*}u(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{\overline{z}}(x_{\Sigma}-y)u(y)\,dy=\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}R_{\overline{z}}u(x_{\Sigma}),\qquad x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma,

and
\Phi_{z}^{*}
gives rise to a bounded operator
\Phi_{z}^{*}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Finally, we introduce a family of boundary integral operators for the Dirac equation. Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
. Then, we define the map
\mathcal{C}_{z}:H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi:=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})\Phi_{z}\varphi,\qquad\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(4.7)

We remark that the operator
\mathcal{C}_{z}
can be represented as a strongly singular boundary integral operator, see for instance
[
9
, equation (4.5) and Proposition 4.4 (ii)]
for the case that
\Omega_{+}
is bounded. However, for our purposes the representation in (
4.7
) is more convenient.
The basic properties of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
are stated in the following proposition. Again, a proof can be found in
[
8
, Appendix C]
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
and let
\mathcal{C}_{z}
be given by (
4.7
). Then, the following is true:

(i)

For any
r\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
the map
\mathcal{C}_{z}
has a bounded extension
\mathcal{C}_{z}:H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

(ii)

For any
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
and
\varphi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
one has

\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi=\pm\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\varphi+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{\pm}.

4.2.
Formulas and corresponding convergence estimates for the resolvents of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
and
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}

In this section we introduce resolvent formulas for
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
and for
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
in terms of (Bochner) integral operators and study their convergence properties.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)=L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and that
W
is the Weingarten map associated to
\Sigma
; cf.
[
8
, Definition 2.3]
and
[
4
, Definition 2.2]
. We (formally) define for
\varepsilon>0
and
z\in\rho(H)
the following integral operators:

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt\begin{aligned} A_{\varepsilon}(z)&:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),\\
A_{\varepsilon}(z)f(x)&:=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 120.0pt\cdot\det(I-\varepsilon sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds,\end{aligned}

(4.8a)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} B_{\varepsilon}(z)&:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),\\
B_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})&:=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 120.0pt\cdot\det(I-\varepsilon sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds,\end{aligned}

(4.8b)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} C_{\varepsilon}(z)&:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),\\
C_{\varepsilon}(z)u(t)(x_{\Sigma})&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y)u(y)\,dy,\end{aligned}

(4.8c)

In the next proposition the well-definedness of these operators and also their connection to the resolvent of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
are discussed.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
q
be as in (
1.4
),
V
be as in (
1.9
),
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
be defined as in (
1.6
) and
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be as below (
1.2
). Then, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
the operators given in (
4.8a
)–(
4.8c
) are well-defined and if
-1\in\rho(B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)
, then
z\in\rho(H_{V_{\varepsilon}})
and the resolvent identity

(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}-z)^{-1}=(H-z)^{-1}-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z)

holds.

Proof.

Note that
\varepsilon_{1}>0
is chosen in exactly the same way as in
[
8
, Proposition 2.4]
. Thus, the assertions follow from
[
8
, (3.1a)–(3.1c), Proposition 3.1 and Proposition 3.2]
.
∎

Next, we introduce the operators
A_{0}(z)
,
B_{0}(z)
, and
C_{0}(z)
which will turn out to be the limit operators of
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
, respectively. For
z\in\rho(H)
, they are defined by

\displaystyle A_{0}(z)

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),

\displaystyle A_{0}(z)f

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{z}\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)\,dt,

\displaystyle B_{0}(z)

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle B_{0}(z)f(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f(s)\,ds+\mathcal{C}_{z}\int_{-1}^{1}f(s)\,ds,

\displaystyle C_{0}(z)

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle C_{0}(z)u(t)

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}u,

where
\Phi_{z}
is the operator defined in
(
4.6
)
and
\mathcal{C}_{z}
is the extension of the operator defined in (
4.7
) to
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see also Proposition
4.3
(i). Using the identifications described in Section
1.1
(
x
), one sees that the operators
A_{0}(z)
,
B_{0}(z)
, and
C_{0}(z)
are well-defined and bounded since by Proposition
4.2
(i)
\Phi_{z}
is a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and by Proposition
4.3
(i)
\mathcal{C}_{z}
is a bounded operator in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. The operator
B_{0}(z)
can also be represented by

B_{0}(z)=T(\alpha\cdot\nu)+\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{I}^{*},

(4.9)

where
\mathfrak{J}
is defined in Section
1.1
(
x
) and

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),\qquad

\displaystyle Tf(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{i}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f(s)\,ds.

Moreover, since
\mathcal{C}_{z}
also acts as a bounded operator in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
r\in[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}]
, see Proposition
4.3
(i),
B_{0}(z)
acts also as a bounded operator in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
for
r\in[-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}]
.

Our next goal is to show a resolvent formula for
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
, which is defined by (
1.7
), in terms of
A_{0}(z),B_{0}(z)
, and
C_{0}(z)
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
q
and
V=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta
be as described in (
1.4
) and (
1.9
), respectively, and
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}
such that (
1.10
) is fulfilled. Moreover, let
(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{\tau})=\textup{tanc}\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)(\eta,\tau)
,
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\eta}I_{N}+\widetilde{\tau}\beta
and
\widetilde{d}=\widetilde{\eta}^{2}-\widetilde{\tau}^{2}
fulfil (
1.12
). If
-1\in\rho(B_{0}(z)Vq)
, then for the self-adjoint operator
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
the resolvent identity

(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)^{-1}=(H-z)^{-1}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)

is valid.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{d}
fulfils (
1.12
),
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
is self-adjoint by the text above (
1.12
). Moreover, in the same way as in
[
8
, below eq. (4.15)]
one can show that if
-1\in\rho(B_{0}(z)Vq)
, then for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
holds

\big((H-z)^{-1}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)\big)u\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}

and

(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)\big((H-z)^{-1}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)\big)u=u.

This shows that the resolvent identity is true.
∎

In the next proposition we summarize the convergence properties of
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
; cf.
[
8
, Proposition 3.7, Proposition 3.8, and Proposition 3.10]
for the proof.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\varepsilon_{1}>0
be as below (
1.2
). Then, there exists an
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
such that
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)

are uniformly bounded operators with respect to
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
. Moreover,
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
and
C_{0}(z)
act also as uniformly bounded operators from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
and for
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
there exists a
C>0
such that one has for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})

\displaystyle{\lVert{A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z)}\rVert}_{0\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},

\displaystyle{\lVert{B_{\varepsilon}(z)-B_{0}(z)}\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},

\displaystyle{\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z)}\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to 0}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.

After summarizing the convergence properties of
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
we state in Proposition
4.7
conditions for the norm resolvent convergence of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
8
, Proposition 3.12 and Remark 4.5]
)
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
4.5
hold. Moreover, let
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and
\varepsilon_{2}>0
be as in Proposition
4.6
. Assume that the following conditions hold:

(i)

There exists an
\varepsilon_{3}\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
such that
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
exists for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{3})
and is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)
.

(ii)

I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is bijective in
\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
.

Then,

{\lVert{(H_{V_{\varepsilon}}-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)^{-1}}\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}

for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{3})
. In particular,
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
converges for
\varepsilon\to 0
to
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
in norm resolvent sense.

According to Proposition
4.7
it is essential to study the operators
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
and
I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq
in order to find explicit conditions for the norm resolvent convergence of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
. In Section
4.3
and Section
4.4
we show that (
4.2
) guarantees that the requirements (i) and (ii) of Proposition
4.7
are met.

4.3.
Analysis of
I+B_{0}(z)Vq

We study the operator
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
and are particularly interested under which conditions (ii) of Proposition
4.7
is fulfilled, i.e. under which conditions
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is bijective in
\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\Sigma)
. First, we state an auxiliary result about the invertibility of
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
with
\mathcal{C}_{z}
defined by (
4.7
), see also Proposition
4.3
(i).

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
,
\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{\tau}\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, and
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\eta}I_{N}+\widetilde{\tau}\beta
such that
\widetilde{d}=\widetilde{\eta}^{2}-\widetilde{\tau}^{2}
fulfils (
1.12
).
Then, the operator
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
is continuously invertible in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Proof.

We split the proof in three steps. In
Step 1
we show that
\widetilde{V}
and
\mathcal{C}_{z}
act as bounded operators in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. Afterwards, we show in
Step 2
that for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
the operator
I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}
is continuously invertible in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. In
Step 3
we use
Step 1 & 2
to prove the assertion.

Step 1.
Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
. The assumption
\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{\tau}\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
implies
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\eta}I_{N}+\widetilde{\tau}\beta\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})\subset W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Hence,
\widetilde{V}
induces a bounded multiplication operator in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
which is bounded by
\|\widetilde{V}\|_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{z}
acts as a bounded operator in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
by Proposition
4.3
(i).

Step 2.
We already know from
Step 1
that
\mathcal{C}_{z}
and
\widetilde{V}
act as bounded operators in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
. Hence, in order to prove that
I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}
is continuously invertible in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, it suffices to show that
I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}
is bijective in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. We begin with the injectivity. Let
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
(I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z})\psi=0
. We set
u=\Phi_{z}\psi
with
\Phi_{z}
defined by (
4.6
). Then, Proposition
4.2
(i) implies
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Furthermore, Proposition
4.2
(ii) yields
(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})u_{\pm}=0
and Proposition
4.3
(ii) gives us

i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})u=\psi\quad\textup{and}\quad\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})u=\mathcal{C}_{z}\psi.

(4.10)

Thus, (
4.10
) leads to

i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})u+\widetilde{V}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})u=\psi+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}\psi=0

(4.11)

and hence
u\in\ker(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)
. Since
\widetilde{d}
fulfils (
1.12
),
H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
is by the text above (
1.12
) self-adjoint and therefore
\ker(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)=\{0\}
; implying
u=0
and therefore (
4.10
) shows
\psi=0
. Now we turn to the surjectivity. Let
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then, according to
[
24
, Theorem 2]
there exist
w_{\pm}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}w_{\pm}=\frac{\mp i(\alpha\cdot\nu)}{2}\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Next, we set
w=w_{+}\oplus w_{-}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and see

i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})w=\varphi\quad\textup{as well as}\quad\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})w=0.

(4.12)

Moreover, let

v:=(H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}-z)^{-1}[(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})w_{+}\oplus(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})w_{-}].

Then,
v\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
and
(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})(v-w)_{\pm}=0
, and thus due to
[
8
, eq. (C.4) and the text below]
there exists a
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
\Phi_{z}\psi=w-v
. Hence, we use the relations (
4.10
) (for
u=\Phi_{z}\psi
) as in (
4.11
), (
4.12
) and
v\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma}}
to obtain

\displaystyle(I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z})\psi

\displaystyle=i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})\Phi_{z}\psi+\widetilde{V}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})\Phi_{z}\psi

\displaystyle=i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})(w-v)+\widetilde{V}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})(w-v)

\displaystyle=i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})w+\widetilde{V}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-})w=\varphi.

This proves the surjectivity and completes
Step 2
.

Step 3.

First, let
r=\tfrac{1}{2}
. In this case we already know from
Step 2
that
I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}
is continuously invertible in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Hence, elementary calculations show that then
I-\mathcal{C}_{z}(I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z})^{-1}\widetilde{V}
is the inverse of
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
. Moreover, it follows from
Step 1 & Step 2
that
I-\mathcal{C}_{z}(I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z})^{-1}\widetilde{V}
is also bounded as an operator in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Consequently, the assertion is true for
r=1/2
. Next, let us consider the case
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
. From the proof of
[
8
, Proposition 2.9]
we obtain that
(\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))^{\prime}
(where
′
is used to denote the anti-dual operator) is a continuous extension of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
to
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Moreover, using the symmetry of
\widetilde{V}
and the fact that
\widetilde{V}
induces a bounded multiplication operator in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
shows that
\widetilde{V}
can also be extended to a bounded multiplication operator in
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Therefore,

\big((I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}})\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)^{\prime}=I+(\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))^{\prime}(\widetilde{V}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))^{\prime}

(4.13)

is a continuous extension of
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
in
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Step 2
shows that
I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}
is continuously invertible in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and therefore the operator in (
4.13
) has the bounded inverse
\big((I+\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}})^{-1}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)^{\prime}
. Hence, one can use interpolation to show the assertion for
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
; cf.
[
8
, eq. (2.2)]
and
[
25
, Theorem B.11]
.
∎

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,
r\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
,
q
and
V=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta
be as described in (
1.4
) and (
1.9
), respectively, and
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}
such that (
1.10
) is fulfilled. Moreover, let
(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{\tau})=\textup{tanc}\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)(\eta,\tau)
,
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\eta}I_{N}+\widetilde{\tau}\beta
and
\widetilde{d}=\widetilde{\eta}^{2}-\widetilde{\tau}^{2}
fulfil (
1.12
).
Then, the operator
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is continuously invertible in the space
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
. In particular, assumption
(ii)
of Proposition
4.7
is fulfilled in this case.

Proof.

We start by arguing that
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is a bounded operator in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
. Due to the representation of
B_{0}(z)
in (
4.9
) and the text below,
B_{0}(z)
acts as a bounded operator in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
. Moreover,
V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and
q\in L^{\infty}((-1,1);[0,\infty))
imply that
Vq
induces a bounded operator in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
, see Section
1.1
(
x
). Hence, it remains to prove the bijectivity of
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
.

Let us start with the injectivity. To do so, we use the representation of
B_{0}(z)
given by (
4.9
) and assume for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)

(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)f=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)f+\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}V\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf=0.

(4.14)

By
[
8
, Lemma 4.2 (i)]

I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq
is continuously invertible in in the space
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
if
\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Note that the structure of
V=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta
and the rules for the Dirac matrices from (
1.18
) imply
((\alpha\cdot\nu)V)^{2}=(\eta^{2}-\tau^{2})I_{N}=dI_{N}
and therefore

\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)V/2)^{2j}}{(2j)!}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{(\sqrt{d}/2)^{2j}}{(2j)!}I_{N}=\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)I_{N}.

Consequently,
\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
since (
1.10
) is satisfied. Hence, we can apply
(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}
to (
4.14
). This yields

f+(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}V\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf=0.

Using
[
8
, Lemma 4.2 (ii)]
and defining
Q(t)=\int_{-1}^{t}q(s)\,ds-\tfrac{1}{2}
,
t\in[-1,1]
, gives us

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{I}=\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{I}

and therefore

f+\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}V\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf=0.

(4.15)

By applying
\mathfrak{I}^{*}q
and
[
8
, Lemma 4.3 (ii)]
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf+\mathfrak{I}^{*}q\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}V\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf

(4.16)

\displaystyle=(I+S\mathcal{C}_{z}V)\mathfrak{I}^{*}qf=0

with
S=\textup{sinc}\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)^{-1}
. Similar as we showed
\cos\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)=\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)I_{N}
one can show
\textup{sinc}\bigl(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\bigr)=\textup{sinc}\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)I_{N}
and therefore

VS=V\textup{tanc}\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)=\widetilde{V}.

(4.17)

Moreover, Lemma
4.8
shows that
-1\in\rho(\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})
. By
[
28
, Proposition 2.1.8]
there holds
\rho(\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\setminus\{0\}=\rho(S\mathcal{C}_{z}V)\setminus\{0\}
. Consequently,
-1\in\rho(S\mathcal{C}_{z}V)
and hence (
4.16
) implies
\mathfrak{J}^{*}qf=0
. This, in turn, implies according to (
4.15
)
f=0
.

Next, we show the surjectivity of the operator
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
. Let
g\in\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
. We set
f_{g}=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}(g+\mathfrak{I}\psi)
, where

\psi=-(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{I}^{*}Vq(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}g.

Item (iii) from
[
8
, Lemma 4.3]
yields together with (
4.17
)

(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{I}=\mathfrak{I}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}).

Thus, by applying
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
to
f_{g}
and using
B_{0}(z)=T(\alpha\cdot\nu)+\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{I}^{*}
, see (
4.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)f_{g}

\displaystyle=(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}g

\displaystyle+(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{I}\psi

\displaystyle=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq+\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{I}^{*}Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}g

\displaystyle+\mathfrak{I}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\psi

\displaystyle=g+\mathfrak{I}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{I}^{*}Vq(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}g+\mathfrak{I}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\psi

\displaystyle=g,

which completes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Analysis of
I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq

In this section we show that if

\sup_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}d(x_{\Sigma})<\frac{\pi^{2}}{4},\qquad d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2},

then (i) of Proposition
4.7
is fulfilled, i.e.
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)
. Combining this result with Proposition
4.7
and Proposition
4.9
proves the norm resolvent convergence of
H_{V_{\varepsilon}}
. To prove the uniform boundedness of
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
a careful and deep analysis of this operator is necessary. We do this by studying
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
in the case that
\Sigma
is a hyperplane and
\eta
and
\tau
are constant in detail in Section
4.4.1
. Then, we use a parameter dependent partition of unity in Section
4.4.2
to transfer the results to the case where

\Sigma=\Sigma_{\zeta,\kappa}
,
\zeta\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
,
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
,
is a rotated
C^{2}_{b}
-graph as in (
4.1
) and
\eta,\tau\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
.
We start by introducing for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
as in Proposition
4.6
the auxiliary operator

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma),

(4.18)

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})

\displaystyle:=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma})f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds;

cf.
[
8
, eq. (3.17), eq. (3.23) and Appendix B]
. According to
[
8
, eq. (3.29)]
,

{\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-B_{\varepsilon}(z)}\rVert}_{0\to 0}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}(1+\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\rvert)^{1/2},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}).

(4.19)

Next, we transfer this operator to
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
.
To do so, we introduce the isomorphism

\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}:L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N}),\quad(\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}f)(x^{\prime}):=f\big(\kappa(x^{\prime},\zeta(x^{\prime}))\big).

(4.20)

By transforming the integral on
\Sigma
to an integral on
\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
one obtains that the norm of
\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}
and its inverse are given by

\displaystyle\|\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}(1+\lvert{\nabla\zeta(x^{\prime})}\rvert^{2})^{-1/4}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\textup{ and }\big\|\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}(1+\lvert{\nabla\zeta(x^{\prime})}\rvert^{2})^{1/4}.

Note that the definition of
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
r\in[0,2]
, see
[
25
]
or
[
8
, Section 2.1]
, implies that
\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}
also acts as an isomorphic operator from
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
for
r\in[0,2]
. Recall that in this case
\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}
can also be viewed as a bounded operator in
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\Sigma)
to
\mathcal{B}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
which has the same norm as the operator acting from
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Next, we introduce for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
the operators

\displaystyle D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)

\displaystyle:=\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}({z})\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}^{-1}:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

(4.22)

\displaystyle\text{and }D_{0}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)

\displaystyle:=\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}B_{0}({z})\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}^{-1}\>:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}).

The results from Proposition
4.6
and (
4.19
) imply that
D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)
is uniformly bounded in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
with respect to
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
and for
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
the inequality

\displaystyle\|D_{0}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)-D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)\|_{1/2\to 0}

\displaystyle=\|\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}\bigl(B_{0}({z})-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}({z})\bigr)\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}^{-1}\|_{1/2\to 0}

(4.23)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|B_{0}({z})-B_{\varepsilon}({z})\|_{1/2\to 0}

\displaystyle+\|B_{\varepsilon}({z})-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}({z})\|_{1/2\to 0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|B_{0}({z})-B_{\varepsilon}({z})\|_{1/2\to 0}

\displaystyle+\|B_{\varepsilon}({z})-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}({z})\|_{0\to 0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\quad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}),

holds.
In particular,
D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)f
converges for
\varepsilon\to 0
to
D_{0}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)f
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{B}^{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
is by
[
20
, Lemma 1.2.19]
and
[
25
, the lines above eq. (3.22)]
a dense subset of
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Combining these considerations with the uniform boundedness of
D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
shows that for all
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)f\to D_{0}^{\zeta,\kappa}(z)f\quad\textup{in }\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}),\textup{ as }\varepsilon\to 0.

(4.24)

Using (
4.18
) and (
4.20
), and setting

x_{\zeta,\kappa}=\kappa(\cdot,\zeta(\cdot))\quad\textup{and}\quad\nu_{\zeta,\kappa}=\nu\circ x_{\zeta,\kappa}=\frac{\kappa(-\nabla\zeta,1)}{\sqrt{1+\lvert{\nabla\zeta}\rvert^{2}}}

(4.25)

yields for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
and a.e.
(t,x^{\prime})\in(-1,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}

\displaystyle D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}({z})f(t)(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}G_{z}\big(x_{\zeta,\kappa}(x^{\prime})-x_{\zeta,\kappa}(y^{\prime})+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu_{\zeta,\kappa}(x^{\prime})\big)

(4.26)

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{1+\lvert{\nabla\zeta(y^{\prime})}\rvert^{2}}f(s)(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}\,ds.

Another useful representation is given by

D_{\varepsilon}^{\zeta,\kappa}({z})f(t)=\int_{-1}^{1}d_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\zeta,\kappa}({z})f(s)\,ds,\quad f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}),t\in(-1,1),

(4.27)

where the integral is considered as a Bochner integral and

\displaystyle d_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}^{\zeta,\kappa}({z})

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N}),

(4.28)

\displaystyle d_{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}^{\zeta,\kappa}({z})g(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}G_{z}\big(x_{\zeta,\kappa}(x^{\prime})-x_{\zeta,\kappa}(y^{\prime})+\widetilde{\varepsilon}\nu_{\zeta,\kappa}(x^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle\cdot\sqrt{1+\lvert{\nabla\zeta(y^{\prime})}\rvert^{2}}g(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime},

for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\in(-2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{2})\setminus\{0\}
. For the interaction strengths
\eta,\tau\in C^{1}_{b}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
we also define the matrix valued function

Q^{\zeta,\kappa}_{\eta,\tau}:=V\circ x_{\zeta,\kappa}=\eta\circ x_{\zeta,\kappa}I_{N}+\tau\circ x_{\zeta,\kappa}\beta.

(4.29)

There holds
Q^{\zeta,\kappa}_{\eta,\tau}=\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}V\iota_{\zeta,\kappa}^{-1}
in the sense of operators in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

4.4.1.
Hyperplanes and constant interaction strengths

In this section we assume that
\zeta
is a constant function having the value
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\zeta,\kappa}=\Sigma_{y_{0},\kappa}
is an affine
(\theta-1)
-dimensional hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
. We also assume in this section that the interaction strengths are constant and given by
\eta,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
. This implies that
Q^{y_{0},\kappa}_{\eta,\tau}
is equal to the constant matrix

Q_{\eta,\tau}:=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta

(4.30)

in this case. The main goal of this section is to show that for every compact set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying

\max_{(\eta,\tau)\in S}\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}<\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}

there exists a
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(S)
such that

\|(I+D^{y_{0},\kappa}_{\varepsilon}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau})^{-1}\|_{0\to 0}

is uniformly bounded with respect to
(\varepsilon,y_{0},(\eta,\tau),\kappa)\in(0,\delta_{2})\times\mathbb{R}\times S\times\textup{SO}(\theta)
; cf. Corollary
4.18
. This result will play a major role when we prove the uniform boundedness of
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
in Section
4.4.2
.
To do so we proceed as follows: We start by using the Fourier transform to transform
D^{y_{0},\kappa}_{\varepsilon}(z)
into a decomposable direct integral operator with frequency dependent fiber operators, see the considerations up to (
4.36
). Then, up to Lemma
4.16
we find and analyse suitable approximations for the fiber operators for high and low frequencies. Finally, we use these results to prove the main statements (Proposition
4.17
and Corollary
4.18
) of this section.

Before we start, let us fix some notations. In the present setting the normal vector
\nu
is constant and given by
\kappa e_{\theta}
, where
e_{\theta}
is the
\theta
-th euclidean unit vector. Let us also mention that
\varepsilon_{2}
from Proposition
4.4
is according to
[
8
, eq. (3.11)]
given by

\varepsilon_{2}=\min\biggl\{\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2},\frac{1}{2{\lVert{D\widetilde{\nu}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}}\biggr\}.

where,
\varepsilon_{1}
is chosen as below (
1.2
), see also
[
8
, Proposition 2.4]
and
\widetilde{\nu}
is an extension
C^{1}_{b}
of
\nu
to
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
; cf.
[
8
, the discussion above eq. (3.5)]
. Next, we argue that
\varepsilon_{2}
can be set independently of
\kappa
to
\infty
. First,
\varepsilon_{1}
is chosen such that one can identify
\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}
via the map
\iota
in (
1.2
) with
\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
and the eigenvalues of
\varepsilon_{1}W(x_{\Sigma})
,
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, are sufficiently small, so that this identification is bijective. However, in the current case
\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}=\kappa(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times(y_{0}-\varepsilon_{1},y_{0}+\varepsilon_{1}))
can be identified with
\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})=\kappa(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times\{y_{0}\})\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
for arbitrary
\varepsilon_{1}>0
and the Weingarten map is zero. Thus, we can set
\varepsilon_{1}
independently of
y_{0}
and
\kappa
to
\infty
. Furthermore, as
\nu
is constant, its constant extension is a
C^{1}_{b}
-extension of
\nu
. Hence,
(2{\lVert{D\widetilde{\nu}}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})})^{-1}=\infty
and therefore
\varepsilon_{2}=\infty
.
Using (
4.26
) and

x_{y_{0},\kappa}(x^{\prime})-x_{y_{0},\kappa}(y^{\prime})+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu_{y_{0},\kappa}(x^{\prime})=\kappa(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime},\varepsilon(t-s)),\,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1},t,s\in(-1,1),

shows that
D_{\varepsilon}^{y_{0},\kappa}(z)
has for
\varepsilon\in(0,\infty)
and
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
the representation

D^{y_{0},\kappa}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x^{\prime})=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}G_{z}\big(\kappa(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime},\varepsilon(t-s))\big)f(s)(y^{\prime})\,dy^{\prime}\,ds

(4.31)

for a.e.
(x^{\prime},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times(-1,1)
, which proves that
D_{\varepsilon}^{y_{0},\kappa}(z)
is independent of
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, (
4.24
) implies that also
D_{0}^{y_{0},\kappa}(z)
is independent of
y_{0}
. Thus, w.l.o.g. we can set
y_{0}=0
. Before we state the next result, we define for convenience the matrices
\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}:=\alpha\cdot\kappa e_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}
,
\widetilde{\alpha}\cdot\xi:=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}\xi_{j}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
, and
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta-1}\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}\xi_{j}^{\prime}
,
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
. Note that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
satisfy the same anti-commutation relations as
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta}
; cf. (
vi
) of Section
1.1
.

We start by calculating the Fourier transform of the function
G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot,\widetilde{\varepsilon}))
for fixed
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\neq 0
; cf.
[
31
, eqs. (44)–(45)]
for similar considerations. Recall that
\mathcal{F}
is the
(\theta-1)
-dimensional Fourier transform defined in Section
1.1
(
xi
).

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
G_{z}
be the integral kernel of
(H-z)^{-1}
given by (
4.4
)–(
4.5
), and
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\neq 0
. Then,

\mathcal{F}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot,\widetilde{\varepsilon}))=\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot(\cdot)+m\beta+zI_{N})}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\cdot}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})\bigg)\frac{ie^{\lvert{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\cdot}\rvert^{2}}}}{2\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1}
,
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}
be defined as in Section
1.1
(
xi
). First, we consider
\mathcal{F}_{1}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))
. Since
G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
, see (
4.4
)–(
4.5
), the expression
\mathcal{F}_{1}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))
is well-defined in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{1}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))=\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))
. Thus, we calculate
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))
next. The function
G_{z}
satisfies the equation
(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})G_{z}=\delta I_{N}
, with
\delta
denoting the
\delta
distribution supported in
\{0\}
. Hence, the standard rules for the Fourier transform, see
[
33
, Chapter IX]
, show

\big(\alpha\cdot(\cdot)+m\beta-zI_{N}\big)\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta}}}I_{N}\quad\textup{in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}).

Furthermore, since
G_{z}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
, we have by the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Using the properties of
\alpha_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}
, and
\beta
yields
(\alpha\cdot\xi+m\beta-zI_{N})^{-1}=\frac{\alpha\cdot\xi+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\lvert{\xi}\rvert^{2}+m^{2}-z^{2}}
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
. Thus,

(\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z})(\kappa\xi)=\frac{\alpha\cdot(\kappa\xi)+m\beta+zI_{N}}{(\lvert{\kappa\xi}\rvert^{2}+m^{2}-z^{2})\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta}}},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

Since
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
, we obtain

\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))(\xi)=(\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z})(\kappa\xi)=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}\cdot\xi+m\beta+zI_{N}}{(\lvert{\xi}\rvert^{2}+m^{2}-z^{2})\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta}}},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

(4.32)

Next, we determine
\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}
. We claim that for a.e.
(\xi^{\prime},x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
the equation

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{1,2}

\displaystyle G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot)))(\xi^{\prime},x_{\theta})

(4.33)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(x_{\theta})\bigg)\cdot\frac{ie^{\lvert{x_{\theta}}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}{2\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}

holds.
We verify (
4.33
) by applying
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and comparing the result with (
4.32
). Since the right hand side of (
4.33
) decays exponentially for
|x_{\theta}|\to\infty
, we can use the integral representation of the Fourier transform. Hence, simple integration gives us for
\xi=(\xi^{\prime},\xi_{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(x_{\theta})\bigg)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{ie^{i(-x_{\theta}\xi_{\theta}+\lvert{x_{\theta}}\rvert\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}})}}{2\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}dx_{\theta}=\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}\cdot\xi+m\beta+zI_{N}}{(\lvert{\xi}\rvert^{2}+m^{2}-z^{2})\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta}}},

which verifies (
4.33
).
Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{1}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))=\mathcal{F}_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{1,2}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))
can be represented by the function

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\ni(\xi^{\prime},x_{\theta})\mapsto\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(x_{\theta})\bigg)\frac{ie^{|x_{\theta}|i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-|\xi^{\prime}|^{2}}}}{2\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}.

Furthermore, since
G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot,\widetilde{\varepsilon}))\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\neq 0
, this shows that for
\widetilde{\varepsilon}\neq 0
and
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
there holds

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot,\widetilde{\varepsilon}))(\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}G_{z}(\kappa(x^{\prime},\widetilde{\varepsilon}))e^{-i\langle x^{\prime},\xi^{\prime}\rangle}\,dx^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1}G_{z}(\kappa(\cdot))(\xi^{\prime},\widetilde{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(\widetilde{\varepsilon})\bigg)\frac{ie^{\lvert{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}{2\sqrt{(2\pi)^{\theta-1}}}.

∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\mathcal{F}
be the
(\theta-1)
-dimensional Fourier transform defined in Section
1.1
(
xi
). Then, there holds for
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}f(t)(\xi^{\prime})=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(t-s)\bigg)

(4.34)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{ie^{\lvert{\varepsilon(t-s)}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}{2}f(s)(\xi^{\prime})\,ds

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}f(t)(\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(t-s)\bigg)

(4.35)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{if(s)(\xi^{\prime})}{2}\,ds

for a.e
(t,\xi^{\prime})\in(-1,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
. In particular, the operators
D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)
and
D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)
depend continuously on
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
with respect to the operator norm.

Proof.

We start with the case
\varepsilon>0
. Equation (
4.31
) shows

D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)f(t)=\int_{-1}^{1}G_{z}\bigl(\kappa(\cdot,\varepsilon(t-s))\bigr)\ast f(s)\,ds

for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
and all
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Thus, Lemma
4.10
and
[
33
, Theorem IX.4]
prove the statement for
\varepsilon>0
. It remains to consider the operator
D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)
. We start by defining

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)

\displaystyle:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{D}_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)f(t)(\xi^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(t-s)\bigg)\frac{if(s)(\xi^{\prime})}{2}\,ds.

Next, let
f\in\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Using (
4.34
) and dominated convergence, see
[
20
, Proposition 1.2.5]
, one obtains that
\mathcal{F}D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}f
converges for
\varepsilon\to 0
to
\widetilde{D}_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)f
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Thus, the boundedness of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
(and therefore also in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
; cf. Section
1.1
(
x
)) implies that
D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)f
converges to
\mathcal{F}^{-1}\widetilde{D}_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}f
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Moreover, by (
4.24
)
D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)f
converges to
D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)f
in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
. Hence,
D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\widetilde{D}_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}f
which proves (
4.35
).

It remains to verify the continuous dependence on
\kappa
. Taking the definition of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
, see the text before Lemma
4.10
, and
D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)
and
D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)
in (
4.34
) and (
4.35
), respectively, into account, one sees that all terms that depend on
\kappa
can be taken out of the integrals. This implies the claimed continuity.
∎

The structure of
\mathcal{F}D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{F}D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}
inspires us to change our viewpoint. Namely, instead of viewing theses operators in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
we consider them as operators in the isometrically isomorphic space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. In
[
34
, Chapter XIII.16]
and generally in the context of direct integrals the notation
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}^{\oplus}L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\,d\xi^{\prime}
for this space is also common.
Considered as operators acting in this space
\mathcal{F}D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{F}D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)\mathcal{F}^{-1}
are decomposable direct integral operators with fibers which are defined for

\varepsilon\geq 0
,
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
, and
z\in\rho(H)
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})

\displaystyle:L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}),

(4.36)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})f(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\frac{\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\xi^{\prime}+m\beta+zI_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(t-s)\bigg)

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{ie^{\lvert{\varepsilon(t-s)}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}{2}f(s)\,ds.

Remark 4.12
.

These operators still depend on the rotation matrix
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
since
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
depend on
\kappa
. However, since we use these operators only as auxiliary operators in this section, we omit
\kappa
.

Next, we explain the ideas described before (
4.36
) in a more rigorous way. Using

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

\displaystyle=L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N}))

\displaystyle\simeq L^{2}((-1,1)\times\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})\simeq L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})),

see
[
20
, Corollary 1.2.23 and Proposition 1.2.24]
, allows us to define the isometric isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}f(\xi^{\prime})(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}f(t)(\xi^{\prime})\quad\text{for a.e. }(\xi^{\prime},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times(-1,1).

Thus, by Proposition
4.11
and (
4.36
) we obtain for
\varepsilon\geq 0
that

\mathfrak{i}D^{0,\kappa}_{\varepsilon}({z})\mathfrak{i}^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}))

acts for
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and fixed
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
as
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})f(\xi^{\prime})
,
i.e.
\mathfrak{i}D^{0,\kappa}_{\varepsilon}({z})\mathfrak{i}^{-1}
is a direct integral operator which can be decomposed in the fiber operators
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})
; cf.
[
34
, Chapter XIII.16]
.

Now, we use the theory of direct integrals in order to transfer results regarding
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})
to
{D}^{0,\kappa}_{\varepsilon}({z})
and vice versa.
We formulate this in the upcoming lemma which follows from
[
34
, Theorem XIII.83 and Theorem XIII.84]
. There, we denote by
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N}))
the space of all bounded operators in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathcal{L}(L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})))
and
M
be defined by

M:\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})\to\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}),\qquad\mathfrak{i}M\mathfrak{i}^{-1}f(\xi^{\prime}):=\mathfrak{M}(\xi^{\prime})f(\xi^{\prime}).

Then,

\displaystyle\|M\|_{0\to 0}

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{M}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathcal{L}(L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})))}

\displaystyle=\textup{ess sup}_{\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}\|\mathfrak{M}(\xi^{\prime})\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

and the operator
M
is continuously invertible if and only if
\mathfrak{M}
is continuously invertible a.e. and the inequality
{\|\mathfrak{M}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathcal{L}(L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})))}<\infty}
holds. Furthermore, in this case
\|M^{-1}\|_{0\to 0}=\|\mathfrak{M}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathcal{L}(L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})))}
.

Next, we study the operator
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}(z)
in detail. For this purpose, we introduce for
\rho\in(0,\infty)
and
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
with
\lvert{w^{\prime}}\rvert=1
the auxiliary operator

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}:L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}f)(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-1}^{1}\big(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}+i\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}\textup{sign}(t-s))\frac{e^{-\rho\lvert{t-s}\rvert}}{2}f(s)\,ds.

It is not difficult to check that
\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}
is a self-adjoint Hilbert-Schmidt operator.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\setminus\{0\}
, and
z\in\rho(H)
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
which only depends on
m
and
z
such that

\displaystyle{\lVert{\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{D}_{0,\xi^{\prime}}({z})}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\varepsilon(1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert),

\displaystyle{\lVert{\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}.

Proof.

In this proof
C>0
denotes a constant which may change in-between lines, but only depends on
m
and
z
. We start by estimating the kernel of the integral operator
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{D}_{0,\xi^{\prime}}({z})
. It is easy to see that we can estimate this kernel by

\displaystyle C\Bigl|1-e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert\sqrt{\lvert{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert).

With this estimate one finds a constant
C_{1}
which only depends on
m
and
z
such that

{\lVert{\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{D}_{0,\xi^{\prime}}({z})}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon(1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert).

Next, we estimate the kernel of
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}
by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\biggl|\left(e^{-\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert|\xi^{\prime}|}-e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i{\sqrt{m^{2}-z^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}\right)\left(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{|\xi^{\prime}|}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}i\textup{sign}(t-s)\right)\biggr|

(4.37)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\Biggl|e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}\Big(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{|\xi^{\prime}|}\Big)\Bigg(1-\frac{i\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}\Bigg)\Biggr|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\Biggl|e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}\Bigg(\frac{m\beta+{z}I_{N}}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}\Bigg)\Biggr|.

The first term in (
4.37
) can be estimated for
\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert\leq 1
by

\displaystyle C\Bigl|e^{-\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert|\xi^{\prime}|}-e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon|t-s|\Big||\xi^{\prime}|+i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{|z^{2}-m^{2}|}{\Big||\xi^{\prime}|-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\Big|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert},

where we used
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus\big((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty)\big)
and
\textup{Im }\sqrt{w}>0
for
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
. For
\varepsilon|t-s|>1
we get

C\Bigl|e^{-\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert|\xi^{\prime}|}-e^{\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert i{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}}\Bigr|\leq C(e^{-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}+e^{-\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}})\leq\frac{C}{1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}.

Similarly as we estimated the first term in the case
\varepsilon\lvert{t-s}\rvert\leq 1
, the second term in (
4.37
) can be estimated by

\displaystyle C\bigg|1-\frac{i\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}}\bigg|

\displaystyle=C\frac{|z^{2}-m^{2}|}{\big|\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}+i|\xi^{\prime}|\big|\big|\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}-\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert^{2}}\big|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+|\xi^{\prime}|)^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{1+|\xi^{\prime}|}.

One also sees that third term in (
4.37
) is smaller than
\tfrac{C}{1+|\xi^{\prime}|}
for sufficiently large
C
. Summing up, we have that the kernel of
\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}
can be bounded by
\tfrac{C}{1+|\xi^{\prime}|}
and therefore if
C_{1}
is chosen sufficiently large, then

{\lVert{\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{1+\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}.

∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\rho>0
,
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
with
\lvert{w^{\prime}}\rvert=1
and
q
be as in (
1.4
). Then,
\sigma(\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q})\subset[-\tfrac{2}{\pi},\tfrac{2}{\pi}]
.

Proof.

To shorten notation we set
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\pm}:=\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}\pm i\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
. Then, one has for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})

\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}f(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t}^{1}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{t}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}f(s)\,ds.

Using the anti-commutation rules for
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
, and
(\widetilde{\alpha}\cdot w^{\prime})^{2}=\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}^{2}=I_{N}
, see the definition of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
before Lemma
4.10
and (
vi
) in Section
1.1
, shows that
\textup{ran }\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}\perp\textup{ran }\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}
. Hence, as
q\geq 0
, we have for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q}f

\displaystyle\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

(4.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}q(t)\bigg|\int_{t}^{1}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}\sqrt{q(s)}f(s)\,ds\bigg|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}q(t)\bigg|\int_{-1}^{t}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}\sqrt{q(s)}f(s)\,ds\bigg|^{2}\,dt.

We start by estimating the first term on the right-hand side. To do so, we assume that
Q
is the primitive function of
q
such that
Q(1)=0
and therefore by
\int_{-1}^{1}q(s)\,ds=1
,
Q(-1)=-1
. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Fubini’s theorem yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}q(t)

\displaystyle\biggl|\int_{t}^{1}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}\sqrt{q(s)}f(s)\,ds\biggr|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}q(t)\biggl|\int_{t}^{1}\frac{\sqrt{\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s)\bigr)}}{\sqrt{\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s)\bigr)}}e^{-\rho|t-s|}\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}\sqrt{q(s)}f(s)\,ds\biggr|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{-1}^{1}q(t)\bigg(\int_{t}^{1}\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s)\bigr)q(s)\,ds\bigg)\bigg(\int_{t}^{1}\frac{1}{\cos(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s))}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)|^{2}\,ds\bigg)\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}-\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(t)\bigr)q(t)\bigg(\int_{t}^{1}\frac{1}{\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s)\bigr)}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)|^{2}\,ds\bigg)\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\int_{-1}^{s}-\sin\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(t)\bigr)q(t)\,dt\bigg)\frac{1}{\cos\bigl(\tfrac{\pi}{2}Q(s)\bigr)}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)|^{2}\,ds.

The same trick with
Q+1
instead of
Q
yields that the second term of the right hand side of equation (
4.38
) can be estimated by
\tfrac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}f(s)|^{2}\,ds.
Using these estimates and
\textup{ran }\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}\perp\textup{ran }\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}
gives us

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q}f\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}|\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}f(s)|^{2}+|\widetilde{\alpha}_{+}f(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}|(\widetilde{\alpha}_{-}+\widetilde{\alpha}_{+})f(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}|2(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime})f(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}{\lVert{f}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}.

Since
\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
, we obtain
\sigma(\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q})\subset[-\tfrac{2}{\pi},\tfrac{2}{\pi}]
.
∎

Having studied the spectrum of
\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q}
, we employ this knowledge to study the bounded invertibility of
I+\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}q
. Recall that
Q_{\eta,\tau}=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta
for
\eta,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\rho>0
,
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
with
\lvert{w^{\prime}}\rvert=1
,
\eta,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
Q_{\eta,\tau}=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta
,
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}
,

c(d):=\begin{cases}\frac{4d}{\pi^{2}},&d\geq 0,\\
0,&d<0,\end{cases}

and
q
be as in (
1.4
).
If
d<\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}
, then
I+\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}q
is continuously invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the norm of the inverse is bounded by the constant

C_{2}=C_{2}(\eta,\tau):=4{\lVert{q}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1))}(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{1+(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{2}{\pi}}{(1-c(d))\pi}+1.

(4.39)

Proof.

Using
\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}=Q_{\eta,-\tau}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}
and
Q_{\eta,-\tau}Q_{\eta,\tau}=dI_{N}
, which follows from the anti-commutation relations for
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{\alpha}_{\theta}
, discussed before Lemma
4.10
, and
\beta
, gives us

I-d(\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q})^{2}=(I-\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q})(I+\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q}).

If
d<\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}
, then Lemma
4.15
implies
1\in\rho(d(\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q})^{2})
and therefore the operator
I+\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q}
is also continuously invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and

\displaystyle\bigl\|(I+\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q})^{-1}\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|(I-d(\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}\sqrt{q})^{2})^{-1}(I-\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q})\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{2}{\pi}}{1-c(d)}.

Moreover,
(I+\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}=I-\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q}(I+\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q})^{-1}\sqrt{q}
and hence

\displaystyle\bigl\|(I+\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle=\bigl\|\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q}(I+\sqrt{q}\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}Q_{\eta,\tau}\sqrt{q})^{-1}\sqrt{q}-I\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq{\lVert{q}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1))}(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert){\lVert{\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}\frac{1+(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{2}{\pi}}{1-c(d)}+1

\displaystyle\leq 4{\lVert{q}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1))}(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{1+(\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert)\frac{2}{\pi}}{(1-c(d))\pi}+1,

where we used Lemma
4.15
(for the constant function
q=\tfrac{1}{2}
) to estimate the term
{\lVert{\mathfrak{H}_{\rho,w^{\prime}}}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}
by
\tfrac{4}{\pi}
.
∎

In the last part of Section
4.4.1
we use our findings to prove norm estimates for the operator
(I+D_{\varepsilon}^{0,\kappa}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
,
\kappa\in\textup{SO}(\theta)
,
\eta,\tau\in\mathbb{R}
such that
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}<\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}
, and
q
be as in (
1.4
). Moreover, let

C_{3}=C_{3}(\eta,\tau,\kappa):=2\max\{C_{2},\|(I+D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}\|_{0\to 0}\},

(4.40)

with
C_{2}=C_{2}(\eta,\tau)
as in (
4.39
), and

\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\eta,\tau,\kappa):=(C_{3}C_{1}(|\eta|+|\tau|){\lVert{q}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1))})^{-2},

(4.41)

with
C_{1}
from Lemma
4.14
. Then,

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\delta_{1})}{\lVert{(I+D^{{0,\kappa}}_{\varepsilon}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}}\rVert}_{0\to 0}\leq C_{3}<\infty.

Proof.

We claim that
C_{3}<\infty
. Since
d<\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}
, the constant
C_{2}
from (
4.39
) is finite. Thus, it suffices to show
\|(I+D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}\|_{0\to 0}<\infty
in order to proof the claim
C_{3}<\infty
.

Applying Proposition
4.9
(for
V=Q_{\eta,\tau}=\eta I_{2}+\tau\beta=\textup{const.}
and
r=0
) shows that
I+B_{0}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q
is continuously invertible in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\Sigma_{0,\kappa})
. Let us shortly explain why Proposition
4.9
is indeed applicable. The inequality
d<\tfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}
implies
d\not=(2k+1)^{2}\pi^{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and therefore (
1.10
) is fulfilled. In the same way as in Proposition
4.9
we set
(\widetilde{\eta},\widetilde{\tau})=\textup{tanc}\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)(\eta,\tau)
. Then,
\widetilde{d}=\widetilde{\eta}^{2}-\widetilde{\tau}^{2}=4\tan\bigl(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\bigr)<4
, i.e.
\widetilde{d}
fulfils (
1.12
). Hence, the assumptions of Proposition
4.9
are satisfied and its application is justified. Together with (
4.21
) and (
4.22
), this implies that the operator
I+D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q=\iota_{0,\kappa}(I+B_{0}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)\iota_{0,\kappa}^{-1}
is continuously invertible in
\mathcal{B}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})
, which proves
\|(I+D_{0}^{0,\kappa}(z)Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}\|_{0\to 0}<\infty
. Hence,
C_{3}<\infty
.

According to Lemma
4.14
we have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})Q_{\eta,\tau}q-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}{\lVert{q}\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1))}\frac{\lvert{\eta}\rvert+\lvert{\tau}\rvert}{1+|\xi^{\prime}|}=\frac{\delta_{1}^{-1/2}}{C_{3}(1+|\xi^{\prime}|)}

for
\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\setminus\{0\}
and all
\varepsilon>0
. Hence, if we choose
R:=\delta_{1}^{-1/2}-1
, then the choices of
C_{3}
,
\delta_{1}
and
R
, and Lemma
4.16
yield for
0\neq|\xi^{\prime}|\geq R
and
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\bigl\|(I+

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}

\displaystyle\cdot(\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})Q_{\eta,\tau}q-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)\bigr\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\frac{\delta_{1}^{-1/2}}{C_{3}(1+R)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{3}}{2}\cdot\frac{\delta_{1}^{-1/2}}{C_{3}\delta_{1}^{-1/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}.

In particular,

\mathfrak{P}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}:=I+(I+\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}(\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})Q_{\eta,\tau}q-\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)

is continuously invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the norm of its inverse can be bounded by
2
for
0\neq|\xi^{\prime}|\geq R
and
\varepsilon>0
. This implies that also

I+\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})Q_{\eta,\tau}q=(I+\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)\mathfrak{P}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}

is continuously invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and by Lemma
4.16
the corresponding norm estimate

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\lVert{(I+\mathfrak{D}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}({z})Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}}\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

(4.42)

\displaystyle=\big\|\mathfrak{P}_{\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}}^{-1}(I+\mathfrak{H}_{\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert\varepsilon,\xi^{\prime}/\lvert{\xi^{\prime}}\rvert}Q_{\eta,\tau}q)^{-1}\big\|_{L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}

is valid for
0\neq|\xi^{\prime}|\geq R
and
\varepsilon>0
.

Having found an estimate for
0\neq|\xi^{\prime}|\geq R
, we aim to find a similar estimate for
0\neq|\xi^{\prime}|\leq R
.
Again, according to Lemma
4.14
we have for
